===== PAGE 1 =====
Unified Fractal Information Geometry of Entropy Operators and
Curvature-Driven Dynamics
A Unified Geometric Principle of Information: Fractal Entropy, Operator Spectra, and Curvature-Driven
Collapse in Multiscale Complex Systems
Stergios Pellis
sterpellis@gmail.com
ORCID iD: 0000-0002-7363-8254
Greece
17 June 2026
Abstract
A unified formulation of information geometry, fractal spectral structure, and entropy-driven dynamics is devel-
oped within the framework of the Information Manifold Theory of Fractal Information, Entropy Projections, and
Curvature-Driven Collapse (PFIT–En-topia–Pellis framework). Information states are represented as points on a
differentiable manifoldMendowed with a Fisher–Rao-type metric extended by multiscale fractal corrections, induc-
ing a scale-dependent geometric structure. Entropy is promoted from a scalar functional to a covariant field defined
onM, whose gradients generate dynamical flows governed by curvature-sensitive evolution equations. Information
propagation is characterized by geodesic motion on the manifold, where deviations from equilibrium correspond
to curvature accumulation and geometric deformation of the statistical state space. A class of entropy projection
operators is introduced, providing a nonlinear mapping from microscopic fluctuations to emergent macrostates via
spectral filtering on the information manifold. These operators generate hierarchical renormalization flows linking
local curvature invariants to global stability properties across scales. Curvature divergence is identified as a universal
mechanism of dynamical collapse, establishing a geometric criterion for critical transitions, decoherence phenomena,
and phase instability in complex multiscale systems. In this regime, scalar curvature singularities correspond to
abrupt structural reorganization of the underlying informational dynamics. The resulting framework establishes a
geometric equivalence between entropy, information flow, and stability structure, unifying fractal spectral dynamics
and curvature-induced collapse within a single manifold-based formalism.
Keywords:Information Manifold Theory , Fractal Information , Entropy Projections , Curvature-Driven Collapse
, Information Geometry , Spectral Dynamics , Multiscale Systems , Renormalization Flows , Critical Transitions ,
Geometric Stability.

1 Introduction
The conceptual evolution of information as a fundamental physical and mathematical entity has progressed through
successive layers of abstraction, beginning with classical communication theory and culminating in contemporary ge-
ometric, spectral, and multiscale formulations. In its original formulation, information was rigorously quantified
as a measure of uncertainty reduction in stochastic communication systems, with entropy emerging as the central
invariant governing optimal encoding, transmission, and inference processes [1–3]. This scalar characterization of
information, while foundational, is inherently limited in its ability to capture structured dependencies, long-range
correlations, and multiscale organization present in complex natural systems. To address these limitations, gener-
alized entropy frameworks were introduced, extending classical additive structures to non-extensive and deformed
statistical regimes capable of modeling systems far from equilibrium. R´ enyi and Tsallis entropies, along with related
generalized information measures, provide a broader functional landscape in which statistical dependence, heavy-
tailed distributions, and anomalous scaling behaviors can be consistently represented [4–6]. These developments
marked a first step toward recognizing entropy not merely as a scalar quantity, but as part of a richer functional
geometry.
A major conceptual advancement arises from the recognition that probability distributions admit a natural
differential geometric structure. In this context, the Fisher–Rao metric endows the space of probability measures with
a Riemannian manifold structure, where statistical distinguishability is encoded in curvature and geodesic distance
[7–9]. Within this framework, inference processes acquire a geometric interpretation as geodesic flows, while curvature
becomes a quantitative descriptor of statistical sensitivity, robustness, and information distinguishability. Parallel
to these developments, nonlinear dynamics and fractal geometry have revealed that complex systems universally
exhibit scale-invariant structure, self-similarity, and hierarchical organization across multiple temporal and spatial
resolutions [10–15]. Such systems cannot be adequately described using classical linear or Euclidean assumptions, as
their intrinsic dynamics are governed by multiscale interactions and emergent critical phenomena. This realization
has led to the incorporation of renormalization group theory as a fundamental analytical tool for describing scale
transformations and universality classes in complex systems [16–21].
From a more abstract functional-analytic perspective, dynamical evolution in both physical and statistical sys-
tems can be formulated in terms of operator flows acting on infinite-dimensional Hilbert or Banach spaces. The
spectral properties of these operators determine stability regimes, resonance structures, and asymptotic convergence
behavior, providing a unifying language for quantum mechanics, statistical physics, and nonlinear dynamical sys-
tems [22–24]. This operatorial viewpoint naturally extends to biological and physiological systems, where fractal
organization and multiscale feedback mechanisms govern functional adaptation and robustness [25–27]. Information-
theoretic divergence measures, particularly those based on relative entropy and Kullback–Leibler structures, introduce
a variational framework in which statistical evolution can be interpreted as a gradient flow on a curved informational
landscape [28, 29]. Within this interpretation, entropy gradients define directional forces of informational descent,
while curvature modulates the stability and anisotropy of such flows. This establishes a deep connection between
thermodynamic irreversibility, statistical inference, and geometric structure.
In modern theoretical physics, these geometric and informational principles have been extended toward at-
tempts to derive physical constants, interaction structures, and cosmological parameters from deeper invariant
principles [30–40]. Such approaches suggest that physical laws may not be fundamental axioms but emergent man-
ifestations of underlying informational or geometric constraints operating at a more primitive level of description.
The incorporation of fractal geometry into this conceptual framework further enhances its expressive power, revealing
that many natural systems exhibit nested hierarchical structures characterized by scale invariance and recursive self-
organization [41–55]. In such systems, macroscopic behavior emerges from iterative transformations of microscopic
rules, leading to effective dynamics that are best described in terms of renormalized or coarse-grained variables.
Biological, neurological, and cognitive systems provide empirical realizations of these principles, where fractal or-
ganization governs neural connectivity, genomic regulation, and physiological variability [56–70]. These observations
strongly indicate that information processing in living systems is inherently multiscale and geometry-dependent, with
functional stability arising from spectral and fractal constraints rather than purely local interactions. In quantum,
gravitational, and cosmological contexts, spectral and operator-theoretic approaches have revealed that stability,
6

===== PAGE 7 =====
phase transitions, and decoherence phenomena may be understood as manifestations of spectral restructuring in
underlying dynamical operators [71–85]. Within this perspective, collapse phenomena correspond not to arbitrary
discontinuities but to structured geometric transitions in the spectral configuration of the system. More recent ad-
vances have extended these ideas into unified frameworks spanning physics, biology, and complex systems theory,
where multiscale spectral instability functions as a universal precursor to critical transitions [86–105]. In such formu-
lations, predictive structure emerges from the analysis of curvature accumulation and spectral concentration across
hierarchical scales.
Finally, a convergent body of theoretical and applied research suggests that curvature singularities, spectral insta-
bility, and fractal renormalization processes collectively define a universal mechanism of collapse and reorganization
in complex systems [106–126]. Within this interpretation, the divergence of geometric invariants on an information
manifold corresponds to macroscopic transitions such as phase changes, decoherence events, biological failure modes,
and systemic instability across engineered networks. Within this context, thePFIT–En-topia–Pellis framework
formalizes a unified differential geometric structure in which entropy, information flow, and stability are not in-
dependent constructs, but emergent projections of a single underlying fractal information manifold endowed with
curvature-driven dynamics, spectral organization, and multiscale renormalization structure.
2 Related Work
The development of a unified informational manifold framework requires a synthesis of information theory, statis-
tical physics, differential geometry, fractal mathematics, and spectral operator theory. Over the last decades, these
domains have converged toward a common structural language in which entropy, curvature, and multiscale dynamics
are treated as mutually dependent objects rather than isolated constructs. The following subsections summarize the
main theoretical pillars underlying the PFIT–En-topia–Pellis framework.
2.1 Foundations of Information Theory and Entropy
The modern theory of information originates from Shannon’s axiomatic framework, in which uncertainty in a
discrete communication system is quantified via the entropy functional [1]. For a probability mass functionp(x)

2.2 Information Geometry and Statistical Manifolds
Information geometry endows the space of probability distributions with a differential-geometric structure, identi-
fying parametric families{p(x|θ)} θ∈Θ as points on a smooth statistical manifoldMequipped with an intrinsic metric
structure [7,9]. In this setting, inference becomes a geometric flow onMrather than a purely algebraic optimization
process.

2.3 Fractal Geometry and Scale Invariance
Fractal geometry, as introduced by Mandelbrot, formalizes the description of structures exhibiting self-similarity
across scales and non-integer dimensionality [10]. In such systems, geometric complexity is not resolved at a single
characteristic scale but distributed across an infinite hierarchy of magnifications, leading to scale-invariant measure-
theoretic structures.

2.4 Critical Phenomena and Renormalization
Critical phenomena establish that systems near phase transitions exhibit universal behavior characterized by scale
invariance and emergent self-similarity, independent of microscopic details [16]. In this regime, physical observables
become governed by fixed-point structures of renormalization group (RG) flows, where coarse-graining transforma-
tions preserve macroscopic statistical structure while systematically integrating out short-range degrees of freedom.
Wilson’s renormalization framework formalizes this scale dependence through flow equations in coupling-
constant space:
dgi(ℓ)
dlnℓ =β i(g),(45)
whereℓdenotes the observation scale andβ i are nonlinear functions encoding the response of effective couplings to
scale transformations. Fixed pointsg ∗ satisfy:
βi(g∗) = 0,(46)
defining universality classes that are invariant under RG transformations.

critical behavior.
Correlation functions near criticality exhibit universal scaling laws. For an order parameter fieldϕ(x), the
two-point correlation function satisfies:
C(r) =⟨ϕ(x)ϕ(x+r)⟩∼r −2∆ϕ,(49)
where ∆ϕ is the scaling dimension of the field. Equivalently, introducing a correlation lengthξ, one obtains:

of system-specific details.

6.4 Commutator Structure and Non-Commutativity

6.7 Interpretation
The PFIT entropy structure is therefore not to be interpreted as a simple scalar hierarchy of information measures,
but rather as a fullygeometrically deformed, curvature-coupled operator algebraacting on a multiscale Hilbert space of
informational states. In this setting, entropy observables are promoted to generators of dynamics on a non-Euclidean
statistical manifold (M,g), where both geometry and probability distributions are mutually interdependent.
More precisely, each entropy operator ˆHα defines a nonlinear functional embedding:

whose action simultaneously modifies amplitude structure, probability densityρ=|ψ| 2, and the induced Fisher–Rao
metricg ij(ρ).

meaning that informational observables fail to commute precisely when the underlying statistical manifold exhibits
curvature or inhomogeneity.
Thus, curvature acts as the structural obstruction to simultaneous diagonalization of entropy channels:
∃ψ∈Hsuch that ˆHαψ=λ αψ∀α⇐⇒R g = 0∧ ∇ρ= 0.(530)
Conversely, renormalization closure reflects stability of informational structure under coarse-graining transfor-
mations:

ensuring that although microscopic operator representations deform with scale, the macroscopic entropy algebra
remains invariant up to reparameterization of its structure coefficients.

7.5 Interpretation
The preceding theorem formalizes a triadic equivalence structure in which informational reality is not reducible
to any single representational layer, but instead emerges as a unified multiscale manifold admitting three mutually
dual realizations: field-theoretic, geometric, and operator-theoretic.
More precisely, the equivalence class structure can be expressed as:
J(x,ω,t)∼(M,g µν(t))∼(H,{H k}),(601)
where each representation is related by nonlinear, scale-dependent isomorphisms preserving normalization, entropy
flow, and spectral consistency up to renormalization corrections.
The interpretation of each layer is therefore as follows:
•J(x,ω,t) is the primal information substrate, encoding the full probabilistic microstate density on the ex-
tended phase–frequency space. Its evolution governs all higher-order structures through nonlinear transport
and entropy production:
∂tJ=N(J,∇J,R g,ℓ fractal),(602)
whereNis a curvature- and scale-coupled nonlinear operator.

•(M,g µν) encodes relational geometry, where the Fisher-type metric emerges as a second-order response func-

gµν = δ2Seff[J]

thereby interpreting geometry as a derived structure of informational sensitivity and distinguishability.
•(H,H k) encodes spectral dynamics, where observables correspond to operator-valued entropy modes acting on
a Hilbert space of informational amplitudes:
Hkψn =λ (k)

so that spectral decomposition encodes stability, collapse, and phase structure of the underlying manifold.
These three levels are not independent constructions but are connected via a nonlinear correspondence functor:

which preserves informational entropy flux up to curvature and fractal corrections:

Consequently, the PFIT–En-topia–Pellis framework identifies informational reality as a single multiscale ge-

observational projection. In the flat and scale-invariant limit, these representations collapse into exact equivalence,
while in the full theory they remain dynamically coupled through curvature, entropy flow, and spectral deformation.

framework. The classical maximum entropy principle is recovered exactly in the flat-manifold limit, while geometric
curvature and fractal information fluctuations generate higher-order spectral corrections that preserve the variational
structure of entropy while extending it into the multiscale informational regime.

entropy from a scalar measure into a geometrically structured spectral field theory on information manifolds.

11 Fundamental Information Field (Ontology)
The PFIT–En-topia–Pellis framework is grounded in the postulate that information is not merely an epistemic
quantity, but a fundamental geometric field defined over a differentiable manifold endowed with fractal structure
and entropy curvature. LetM I denote the informational manifold, where each state is represented by a generalized
probability densityp(x;θ) together with a scale-dependent entropy functionalS[·]. The ontological assumption is
that physical, biological, and computational systems emerge as projections of this underlying informational field.

12.3 Curvature as Instability Indicator

12.4 Entropy–Curvature Feedback Loop

manifold (M,gij).

13 Information Manifold Geometry
The geometric structure underlying the PFIT–En-topia–Pellis framework is formulated on a differentiable statisti-
cal manifoldM I endowed with a dual metric structure induced by entropy functionals and fractal scaling operators.
This manifold encodes probability distributions as geometric points, while information-theoretic observables emerge
as intrinsic curvature invariants of the underlying space. The resulting geometry is neither purely Riemannian nor
purely Euclidean but belongs to a class of entropy-curved, scale-dependent statistical manifolds.

14 Fractal Information Structure (PFIT Formulation)
The PFIT formulation introduces a geometric re-interpretation of information as a scale-dependent field defined on
a fractal information manifold. In this framework, information is not a static functional but a dynamically evolving
object governed by curvature, entropy flow, and renormalization across nested observational scales. The central
objective of this section is to formalize the emergence of fractal information structure from entropy-driven geometry
and to establish its role as the foundational layer of the unified PFIT–En-topia–Pellis framework, consistent with
generalized information geometry and multiscale statistical physics [1–3, 7, 8, 16–18, 82]. The key conceptual shift is
the replacement of classical probability spaces with scale-coupled fractal manifolds, where both the measure and the
metric depend explicitly on resolution. This leads naturally to curvature-induced informational phase transitions
and a renormalization-driven collapse mechanism that unifies entropy maximization with geometric instability.
14.1 Fractal Measure-Theoretic Information Structure

measure-theoretic structureµ F, which replaces the classical Lebesgue volume element with a scale-dependent
geometric–informational measure encoding multiscale heterogeneity and self-similarity.

14.5 Connection to Pellis Fractal Information Principle
Within the PFIT–En-topia–Pellis framework, the fractal information structure is fully consistent with the universal
formulation of fractal information theory introduced in [82], in which information is not treated as a passive statistical
descriptor but as aself-organizing generative fieldconstrained by recursive geometric and entropic consistency
conditions across scales.
In this interpretation, informational coherence emerges as a variational balance between entropy curvature
and geometric stability. This balance is sharply characterized by a marginal stability condition in which curvature
fluctuations are dynamically suppressed while entropy curvature is extremized:
δRI≈0, δ 2SF→min,(1260)
defining a universal critical regime in which the system resides at the boundary between ordered geometric
coherence and fractal instability.
This condition can be interpreted as a constrained extremization principle:
δ[SF−λRI] = 0,(1261)
implying that optimal informational configurations arise from a competition between entropy maximization and
curvature regularization.
At the level of fluctuations, stability is governed by the second variation functional:

whereH S denotes the entropy Hessian operator encoding the local response of the system to perturbations in
informational density.

===== PAGE 131 =====
indicating the emergence of zero modes associated with scale-invariant fractal structures and long-range informa-
tional correlations.
In this regime, curvature fluctuations satisfy a constraint equation of the form:
RI∼∇ 2SF +γVar(I),∇R I→0,(1264)
implying homogenization of curvature gradients at the critical point while preserving large-amplitude fluctuations
in higher-order moments.
The Pellis principle therefore identifies fractal information as a fundamental geometric field governed by a
coupled entropy–curvature–renormalization dynamics:
(SF,RI,Rℓ)←→closed multiscale dynamical system,(1265)
where each component recursively generates the others through scale-dependent feedback loops. Consequently,
fractal information is not an emergent statistical artifact but aprimary geometric degree of freedom, in which
entropy flow determines curvature, curvature regulates stability, and renormalization governs self-similar organization
across all scales ofM I, consistent with the foundational structure of [82].
15 Multiscale Derived Fields (PFIT, En-topia, Spectral)

information manifoldMI. This perspective unifies entropy geometry, spectral theory, and nonlinear collapse dynam-
ics into a single multiscale generative structure consistent with renormalization principles and information-geometric
foundations [1–3,7,8,16–18,25,27,82].

16 Projection Operator Framework
The Projection Operator Framework constitutes the operational bridge between the full fractal–spectral infor-
mation manifoldM I and the experimentally accessible macroscopic observables. Within the PFIT–En-topia–Pellis
formalism, physical, biological, and computational measurements are interpreted as nonlinear coarse-grained projec-
tions of a higher-dimensional informational state space. This section formalizes the projection structure, establishes
its operator algebra, and demonstrates how curvature- and entropy-driven collapse emerge naturally from projection
instability across scales [1–3,7,8,16–18,82].

17 Entropy as Geometric Projections

18 Dynamical Evolution Law
The dynamical backbone of the PFIT–En-topia–Pellis framework is constructed as a geometric evolution theory
of information fields on a nonlinear, multiscale, and fractally deformed manifold of states. Unlike classical stochastic
or thermodynamic evolution laws, the present formulation treats temporal evolution as an intrinsic deformation
of informational geometry, where entropy production, curvature flow, and fractal renormalization are inseparably
coupled. In continuity with the universal fractal information program established in [82], the dynamical law emerges
as a unified operatorial structure governing both micro- and macro-scale informational transitions.

20 Axiomatic Foundation
The PFIT–En-topia–Pellis framework is formulated as an axiomatic reconstruction of information dynamics on a
curved fractal manifold, where entropy, geometry, and scale invariance are treated as co-primitive structures rather
than derived quantities. In alignment with the universal fractal information hypothesis introduced in [82], the theory
is grounded on a minimal set of axioms that uniquely determine the coupled evolution of informational density and
geometric structure.

21 Entropy & Fractal Unification

the universal fractal information program introduced in [82] into a rigorous theorem governing structural equivalence
classes on the information manifold.

Within the PFIT–En-topia–Pellis framework, entropy geometry and fractal information geometry are not merely

24 Cross-Domain Applications
This section extends the PFIT–En-topia–Pellis unified information manifold framework into a broad class of
physical, biological, and engineered systems exhibiting multiscale instability, fractal organization, and entropy-driven
phase transitions. The central hypothesis is that curvature-driven informational collapse constitutes a universal
organizing principle across domains, consistent with geometric entropy theory, spectral instability analysis, and
fractal information dynamics [1,2,7,8,13,14,16,18,25,28,82].
24.1 General Universality Principle
LetXdenote a generic dynamical system endowed with state spaceSand evolution operatorU t. The universality
postulate asserts the existence of a structure-preserving embedding
Φ :X→(M,g ij,ρ),(1941)
such that macroscopic observables, correlation structures, and dynamical invariants ofXare represented as geometric,
spectral, and entropic fields on a fractal information manifold equipped with curvature and measure structure.
More precisely, the embedding Φ is assumed to satisfy a functorial consistency condition with respect to time
evolution:
Φ◦U t ≈ Ft◦Φ,(1942)
whereFt denotes the induced nonlinear geometric flow on (M,gij,ρ), ensuring dynamical equivalence up to admissible
renormalization errors.
Under this embedding, the system evolution is governed by a curvature–entropy driven flow of the form
d
dtΦ(X) =∂ tρ=C[ρ] +R[g ij] +E[ρ,g ij],(1943)

27 Limitations and Model Assumptions
This section rigorously formalizes the structural assumptions, asymptotic regimes, and mathematical idealiza-
tions underlying the PFIT–En-topia–Pellis framework. Although the theory is constructed as a unified geomet-
ric–informational description of collapse phenomena across scales, its validity is intrinsically conditioned on a hi-
erarchy of smoothness, integrability, spectral regularity, and renormalization constraints consistent with modern
entropy geometry, stochastic analysis, and fractal information manifolds [1–3, 7–9, 28, 82]. These constraints define
the admissible class of systems for which curvature-driven collapse remains mathematically well-posed.

27.9 Summary of Limitations
The PFIT–En-topia–Pellis framework is therefore best interpreted as an asymptotic, renormalized theory of
informational geometry valid under smoothness, finite entropy, controlled fractal scaling, and spectral self-adjointness
assumptions. Its predictive strength emerges in regimes where entropy, curvature, and spectral dynamics remain
coherently coupled, while its validity weakens in fully singular, non-measurable, or non-renormalizable informational
states [1–3,28,82].
28 Future Work
The PFIT–En-topia–Pellis framework establishes a geometric–informational foundation for multiscale collapse
dynamics across entropy, spectral, and curvature-driven regimes. Nevertheless, it remains an open, evolving math-
ematical structure whose deepest properties—particularly its renormalization closure, quantum embedding, and
computational realizability—are not yet fully determined. Future developments are expected to refine its axiomatic
core, extend its fractal-geometric consistency classes, and establish a unified treatment of stochastic, quantum, and
biological information manifolds within a single curvature–entropy duality principle [1–3,7–9,28,82].

heterogeneous dynamical processes onto a single curvature–entropy manifold structure, thereby enabling predictive
modeling of critical transitions across physical, biological, computational, and social domains [1–3,7–9,28,82].

30 Positioning within Existing Entropy and Geometric Theories
To clarify the conceptual and mathematical positioning of the PFIT framework, we explicitly compare its structure
against the main existing entropy, information-theoretic, and geometric formulations in the literature. This section is
not merely descriptive but establishes the precise regime in which PFIT extends, unifies, and generalizes prior theories
through an operator–geometric closure principle on information manifolds, in which entropy production, curvature
evolution, and spectral instability are treated as mutually coupled dynamical degrees of freedom [1–3,7–9,28,82].

31 Discussion
The PFIT–En-topia–Pellis framework establishes a unifying geometric–informational paradigm in which entropy,
curvature, and fractal structure are not independent descriptors but mutually coupled manifestations of a single
underlying manifold dynamics. The central conceptual advance lies in the identification of curvature as the primary
driver of informational instability, extending classical information geometry into a nonlinear, multiscale, and fractal
regime consistent with entropy projection mechanisms and collapse dynamics [1–3,7–9,28,82].
More formally, the system can be viewed as an operator–variational triple (M,g,C) governed by a coupled
flow:

whereGandK ij encode curvature–entropy backreaction terms.

curvature tensors. This is formalized through the asymptotic relation

31.7 Final Perspective
Overall, the PFIT–En-topia–Pellis framework suggests that complex systems across disciplines are governed by
a single underlying variational principle linking entropy maximization, curvature evolution, and fractal scaling. In
compact operator form, this is expressed as
DPFIT =⟨H entropy +H curvature +H spectral⟩RG,(2210)
implying that diverse phenomena—from physical phase transitions to biological instability and computational break-
down—emerge as projections of a universal geometric–informational law [82,101–104,114–117].

32 Conclusion
The Unified Information Manifold Theory of Fractal Information, Entropy Projections, and Curvature-Driven
Collapse (PFIT–En-topia–Pellis Framework) establishes a coherent and mathematically structured synthesis of in-
formation geometry, fractal dynamics, and nonlinear curvature-driven evolution. The central thesis is that informa-
tional systems are fundamentally described not as static probabilistic ensembles, but as evolving geometric–operator
manifolds (M,gij(t),ρ(x,t)) whose curvature, spectral structure, and entropy flow are mutually coupled dynamical
degrees of freedom [1–3,7–9,28,82].
In this formulation, system evolution is governed by a coupled nonlinear geometric flow:
